Covariant Spinor Formalism for Multipole Expanded Form Factor


Abstract
We present a systematic technique for constructing Lorentz covariant orbital-spin ( L ​ S LS ) bases for matrix elements of local operators and the associated form factors, thereby extending the traditional multipole expansion to a Lorentz covariant formalism. In the spinor-helicity formalism, matrix elements of local operators for spin- j j particles can be treated as several massive 3-point scattering amplitudes, each of which can be further decomposed into different L ​ S LS partial wave amplitudes. We obtain explicit complete and linearly independent L ​ S LS amplitude bases for scalar, vector, and rank 2 tensor form factor of particles with spin- 1 2 \frac{1}{2} , 1 1 , and 3 2 \frac{3}{2} . In the Breit frame, it recovers the traditional multipole expansion expression, and we show the explicit equivalence among the traditional multipole expansion, canonical L ​ S LS expansion, and the SO ⁡ ( 3 ) \mathrm{SO}(3) Zemach tensor expansion. Finally noting covariant structures built from the relativistic external wave functions and momenta of the initial and final state particles, we give a universal construction formula for form factor of arbitrary Lorentz tensor operators for arbitrary external spin particles.

1
Introduction

Form factors (FFs) are among the standard observables used to describe the internal structures and interactions of composite particles. They arise when matrix elements of local operators between one particle states are expanded in a basis of independent tensor or spinor structures, with Lorentz invariant scalar coefficients identified as FFs. Typical examples are provided by the electromagnetic current and the energy-momentum tensor (EMT), whose matrix elements define electromagnetic and gravitational FFs and thereby encode quantities such as charge, magnetization, momentum, angular momentum, and other mechanical characteristics of the system
[
44
,
2
,
40
,
36
,
37
,
6
,
34
]
. In the context of QCD, where quarks and gluons are not directly observed because of confinement, such FFs serve as especially important probes of nonperturbative hadron structure
[
41
,
7
,
40
,
31
,
29
]
.

Most of the early literature on matrix element decompositions concentrated on low spin targets, in particular spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
systems such as the proton
[
44
,
2
,
40
,
6
,
36
]
, with substantial extensions later developed for spin-
1
particles
[
20
,
5
,
22
,
23
,
47
,
4
,
42
,
12
,
39
]
. In recent years, attention has increasingly turned to higher spin states, including spin-
\tfrac{3}{2}

[
35
,
38
,
28
]
, spin-
2
, and eventually arbitrary spin cases
[
26
,
32
,
30
,
14
,
39
,
27
,
46
]
. Correspondingly, a number of counting rules have been derived for scalar, vector, and rank two tensor operators acting on particles of various spin
[
45
,
50
,
14
,
13
,
33
,
46
]
. Beyond counting, explicit covariant constructions of matrix element bases have also been developed. Besides case by case parameterizations for specific particles or specific processes, systematic methods have been proposed for constructing bases for particles of arbitrary spin and for operators in general Lorentz representations. For example, the spinor Young tableaux method provides an algorithmic construction of Lorentz covariant tensor structures and has been used to obtain complete bases for particles with spin up to
2

[
46
]
. Taken together, these studies have significantly broadened the available toolkit for explicit covariant parameterizations of local operator matrix elements.

Within such covariant constructions, the same matrix element can be expressed using different choices of spin quantization axes for the external particles, which can highlight different properties of composite particles. For example, the intrinsic degrees of freedom of the external particles may be represented in the helicity basis or in the canonical spin basis
[
25
,
3
,
49
,
10
,
9
,
8
,
11
]
. The canonical spin basis is particularly useful for the present purpose, because the traditional
LS
partial wave expansion and the conventional low energy multipole expansion should be formulated in terms of canonical spin states.
However, these traditional formulations are not manifestly Lorentz covariant. Therefore, by themselves, they do not provide a sufficiently systematic framework for constructing complete covariant bases of general local operator matrix elements. In this work, we extend the
LS
coupling construction to a Lorentz covariant setting with canonical spin states. When the matrix element basis is organized by the
LS
coupling, the canonical spin construction reduces in the Breit frame to the familiar
LS
partial wave form and also matches naturally onto the conventional low energy multipole expansion. In this sense, our construction can be regarded as a covariant extension of the
LS
coupling description

To make this
LS
organization explicit, we use an organizing principle familiar from conventional angular momentum analyses, namely to regard the operator insertion as the third object in a three point structure. In this form, the basis can be classified by the coupling of the orbital angular momentum
L
and the total spin
S
of the external particles. The orbital part is carried by the relative momentum of external particles, while the spin part is carried by the little group degrees of freedom of the external particles. The two parts are coupled to a fixed total angular momentum by Clebsch-Gordan coefficients (CGCs). Each allowed
(L,S)
channel then gives one independent angular momentum structure, whose coefficient is the corresponding form factor.

This construction is closely connected with several traditional methods based on angular momentum. On the one hand, the
LS
coupling viewpoint provides a useful way to compare our construction with the conventional multipole expansion
[
16
,
15
,
18
,
17
,
21
,
48
,
19
]
. In the
LS
basis, a matrix element is organized by first introducing the orbital part, which carries the momentum dependence, and the spin part, which is obtained by coupling the spin degrees of freedom of the initial and final states. These two parts are then coupled to the total
\mathrm{SO}(3)
angular momentum
J
carried by the operator component.
In tensor formulations, the same idea is usually implemented by first constructing orbital and spin tensors in the three dimensional rotation group, as in the Zemach tensor method
[
51
,
52
]
.
By contrast, the multipole expansion follows a different ordering. The current is first decomposed into the charge density and the spatial current, and the corresponding matrix elements are then expanded into Coulomb, longitudinal, electric, and magnetic multipoles
C_{K},L_{K},E_{K},M_{K}
. This comparison shows that the common point of these two constructions is that the multipole order
K
is equivalent to the total spin
S
of the spin part in the
LS
basis, which is also built from the initial and final spin states. In terms of the correspondence between the two bases,
C_{K}
and
M_{K}
each correspond to the single
LS
channel
(L,S)=(K,K)
, whereas
L_{K}
and
E_{K}
for
K\geq 1
are two fixed orthogonal linear combinations of
(K-1,K)
and
(K+1,K)
. Thus, the conventional multipole basis classifies the current by its charge, longitudinal, electric, and magnetic components, while the
LS
basis classifies the same structures by their orbital angular momentum and total spin.

However, the traditional
LS
expansion is not covariant. It is usually defined in the center-of-mass (COM) frame. Once a boost is performed, different
LS
components can mix with each other. Therefore, a covariant
LS
method is needed, so that the
LS
basis can be defined in an arbitrary reference frame. This motivates the canonical-spinor method
[
24
]
.
This approach has several advantages. To begin with, it is simple and algorithmic, because the essential structural information has already been encoded in the amplitude basis, so one only needs to carry out the prescribed conversion. Moreover, no separate redundancy removal step is required, since completeness and linear independence are inherited from the three point amplitude basis itself. The resulting basis is also fully general and does not rely on any assumption of
P
or
T
conservation. Finally, the counting of independent structures becomes straightforward as well with the same
LS
coupling analysis.

Following this strategy, we construct complete and linearly independent bases for matrix elements with arbitrary initial and final spins and with operator insertions in arbitrary Lorentz representations
(j_{L},j_{R})
. These bases can be written explicitly, and their counting follows directly from the allowed
LS
couplings. For application, we present the construction results in a more concrete form for the cases in which the external particles carry spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
, spin-
1
, and spin-
\tfrac{3}{2}
. As a supplement, the general construction for arbitrary spin and arbitrary representations is also presented in the appendix for completeness. Finally, we compare our results with the recent covariant construction of Ref.
[
46
]
, and find redundant structures in the proposed spin-
1
vector and rank-2 tensor bases. Altogether, these results provide a systematic covariant multipole expansion for generalized FFs that is explicit, complete, and free of hidden redundancies.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In section
2
, we have reviewed the conventional electromagnetic multipole expansion and compared it with the
LS
expansion and the Zemach
LS
expansion. In section
3
develops the canonical-spinor formulation, explains how the complete three point
LS
amplitude basis is converted into a basis of matrix elements, and derives the kinematic constraints implied by the
LS
coupling. The subsequent sections present explicit results for external particles of spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
, spin-
1
, and spin-
\tfrac{3}{2}
.

2
Multipole expansion and canonical
LS
expansion

2.1
Traditional multipole expansion

The traditional electromagnetic multipole expansion starts from the separation of the electromagnetic current into the charge density and the spatial current,

J^{\mu}(\mathbf{x})=(\rho(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{x})),

(2.1)

where
\rho(\mathbf{x})
is the charge density and
\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{x})
is the spatial current density. Under spatial rotations,
\rho(\mathbf{x})
behaves as a scalar, whereas
\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{x})
behaves as a vector.

The momentum space current is obtained through the three-dimensional Fourier transformation,

J^{\mu}(\mathbf{q})=\int d^{3}x\,e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}J^{\mu}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.2)

Equivalently, the charge density and the spatial current are transformed separately,

\rho(\mathbf{q})=\int d^{3}xe^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\rho(\mathbf{x}),\qquad\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{q})=\int d^{3}x\,e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.3)

The multipole expansion follows from expanding the plane wave into spherical waves,

e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}j_{k}(qx)Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{x}),

(2.4)

where
j_{k}(qx)
is the spherical Bessel function and
Y^{k}_{m}
is the spherical harmonic. This formula separates the dependence on the direction of the momentum transfer,
\hat{q}
, from the dependence on the coordinate direction,
\hat{x}
. In the radial integrals below,
q=|\mathbf{q}|
and
x=|\mathbf{x}|
.

First, consider the charge density part. Substituting the plane wave expansion in Eq. (
2.4
) into Eq. (
2.3
), one obtains

\rho(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})\int d^{3}xj_{k}(qx)Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{x})\rho(\mathbf{x}).

(2.5)

Then, The charge density multipoles can be defined as

C^{k}_{m}(q^{2})=4\pi i^{k}\int d^{3}xj_{k}(qx)Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{x})\rho(\mathbf{x}).

(2.6)

Thus the charge density in momentum space can be written as

\rho(\mathbf{q})=\sum_{k,m}Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})C^{k}_{m}(q^{2}).

(2.7)

This is the charge multipole expansion. The angular dependence is
carried by the spherical harmonic
Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})
, while the dynamical
information is contained in the charge density multipoles
C^{k}_{m}(q^{2})
.
In the comparison below, the common normalization factor
4\pi i^{k}
will be
suppressed for notational simplicity and absorbed into the reduced multipole
coefficient
C^{k}_{m}
.

For the spatial current, the Fourier transformation gives

\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{q})=\int d^{3}x\,e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.8)

Different from the charge density,
\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{q})
carries a Cartesian vector index. It can therefore be decomposed into components longitudinal and transverse to the momentum-transfer direction
\hat{q}
. The transverse part contains two independent angular structures, conventionally called electric and magnetic. This motivates the introduction of the vector spherical harmonics used below.

A convenient vector harmonic basis is

Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{km}(\hat{q})=\hat{q}^{i}Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{q}),

(2.9)

Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{km}(\hat{q})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k(k+1)}}q\nabla^{i}Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{q}),

(2.10)

and

Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{km}(\hat{q})=\epsilon^{ijr}\hat{q}^{j}Y^{(\mathrm{E})r}_{km}(\hat{q})=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{k(k+1)}}(\mathbf{q}\times\boldsymbol{\nabla})^{i}Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{q}).

(2.11)

Here
\nabla^{i}_{q}=\partial/\partial q^{i}
. Since

\hat{q}_{i}Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{km}(\hat{q})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k(k+1)}}\hat{q}_{i}q\nabla_{q}^{i}Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{q})=0,

(2.12)

and

\hat{q}_{i}Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{km}(\hat{q})=\epsilon^{ijr}\hat{q}_{i}\hat{q}_{j}Y^{(\mathrm{E})r}_{km}(\hat{q})=0.

(2.13)

Thus the electric and magnetic vector harmonics are transverse to the
momentum transfer direction, while
Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{km}
is longitudinal. For
k=0
, the ordinary spherical harmonic is a constant, so only the longitudinal branch survives.

We now decompose the spatial current into longitudinal and transverse
parts,

J^{i}(\mathbf{q})=J^{(\mathrm{L})i}(\mathbf{q})+J^{(\mathrm{E})i}(\mathbf{q})+J^{(\mathrm{M})i}(\mathbf{q}).

(2.14)

The longitudinal component is obtained by projecting the current onto the
direction of the momentum transfer,

J_{L}(\mathbf{q})=\hat{q}^{i}J^{i}(\mathbf{q}).

(2.15)

Using

\nabla_{x}^{i}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}=iq^{i}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}},\qquad\nabla^{i}_{x}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},

(2.16)

one has

\hat{q}^{i}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}=-\frac{i}{q}\nabla^{i}_{x}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}.

(2.17)

Using Eq. (
2.4
), we obtain

\hat{q}^{i}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{x}}=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})\left[-\frac{i}{q}\nabla^{i}_{x}\left(j_{k}(qx)Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{x})\right)\right].

(2.18)

Therefore

J_{L}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})L^{k}_{m}(q^{2}),

(2.19)

where the longitudinal multipole is defined by

L^{k}_{m}(q^{2})=-\frac{i}{q}\int d^{3}x\left\{\nabla^{i}\left[j_{k}(qx)Y^{k}_{m}(\hat{x})\right]\right\}J^{i}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.20)

This expression gives the scalar longitudinal component. The corresponding
longitudinal vector current is obtained by multiplying back
\hat{q}^{i}
,

J^{(\mathrm{L})i}(\mathbf{q})=\hat{q}^{i}J_{L}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}\hat{q}^{i}Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})L^{k}_{m}(q^{2}).

(2.21)

Using Eq. (
2.9
), this becomes

J^{(\mathrm{L})i}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}Y^{(\mathrm{L})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})L^{k}_{m}(q^{2}).

(2.22)

The transverse current contains two independent structures. In the
conventional electromagnetic multipole expansion, they are called the
electric and magnetic transverse multipoles. With the present notation, the
magnetic multipole is defined by projecting the current onto the magnetic
vector spherical wave,

M^{k}_{m}(q^{2})=\int d^{3}xj_{k}(qx)Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{km}(\hat{x})J^{i}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.23)

The electric multipole is defined by the curl of the magnetic vector
spherical wave,

E^{k}_{m}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{q}\int d^{3}x\left\{\nabla_{x}\times\left[j_{k}(qx)\mathbf{Y}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{km}(\hat{x})\right]\right\}^{i}J^{i}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.24)

Equivalently,

E^{k}_{m}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{q}\int d^{3}x\epsilon^{ijr}\nabla^{j}_{x}\left[j_{k}(qx)Y^{(\mathrm{M})r}_{km}(\hat{x})\right]J^{i}(\mathbf{x}).

(2.25)

After the electric and magnetic multipole coefficients have been extracted,
the transverse vector current is reconstructed by multiplying them back by
the corresponding transverse vector harmonics. The electric part is

J^{(\mathrm{E})i}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}Y^{(\mathrm{E})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})E^{k}_{m}(q^{2}),

(2.26)

and the magnetic part is

J^{(\mathrm{M})i}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}Y^{(\mathrm{M})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})M^{k}_{m}(q^{2}).

(2.27)

Therefore the full transverse current is

J^{(\mathrm{T})i}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}\left[Y^{(\mathrm{E})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})E^{k}_{m}(q^{2})+Y^{(\mathrm{M})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})M^{k}_{m}(q^{2})\right].

(2.28)

Combining the longitudinal and transverse parts, the full spatial current is

J^{i}(\mathbf{q})=J^{(\mathrm{L})i}(\mathbf{q})+J^{(\mathrm{E})i}(\mathbf{q})+J^{(\mathrm{M})i}(\mathbf{q}).

(2.29)

Thus

J^{i}(\mathbf{q})=4\pi\sum_{k,m}i^{k}\left[Y^{(\mathrm{L})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})L^{k}_{m}(q^{2})+Y^{(\mathrm{E})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})E^{k}_{m}(q^{2})+Y^{(\mathrm{M})*i}_{km}(\hat{q})M^{k}_{m}(q^{2})\right].

(2.30)

This is the conventional electromagnetic multipole expansion of the spatial current. The vector spherical harmonics carry the angular dependence in
\hat{q}
, while
L^{k}_{m}(q^{2})
,
E^{k}_{m}(q^{2})
, and
M^{k}_{m}(q^{2})
contain the longitudinal, transverse electric, and transverse magnetic multipole information.

The conventional expansion above is an operator level decomposition of the electromagnetic current. For form factor matrix elements, these operators are inserted between one-particle states with spin-
j
. Their matrix elements therefore carry the external spin labels of the final and initial states.

For example, the charge multipole operator gives

C^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};ja_{1}|C^{k}_{m}(q^{2})|p_{3};ja_{3}\rangle,

(2.31)

and similarly for the longitudinal, electric, and magnetic multipoles,

X^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};ja_{1}|X^{k}_{m}(q^{2})|p_{3};ja_{3}\rangle,\qquad X=C,L,E,M.

(2.32)

Thus, after taking matrix elements, the conventional multipole coefficients are no longer only functions of the momentum transfer. They also form matrices in the spin space of the external particles.

2.2
Canonical
LS
expansion

We consider the elastic matrix element of a local operator
\mathcal{O}^{\mu\nu\cdots}
between one-particle states with the same spin
j
,

\mathcal{M}^{\mu\nu\cdots}(p_{1},p_{3};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};ja_{1}|\mathcal{O}^{\mu\nu\cdots}|p_{3};ja_{3}\rangle.

(2.33)

In this convention,
p_{3}
labels the initial one-particle state and
p_{1}
labels the final one-particle state. The indices
a_{3}
and
a_{1}
denote the corresponding canonical spin projections. Both external particles are taken on shell, so that
p_{1}^{2}=p_{3}^{2}=m^{2}
. We also introduce the average momentum and momentum transfer as

P^{\mu}=\frac{p^{\mu}_{1}+p^{\mu}_{3}}{2},\qquad Q^{\mu}=p^{\mu}_{3}-p^{\mu}_{1}.

(2.34)

To make the
\mathrm{SO}(3)
decomposition explicit, we work in the Breit frame. The average momentum and momentum transfer can be written as

P^{\mu}=\frac{p^{\mu}_{1}+p^{\mu}_{3}}{2}=(E,0,0,0),\qquad Q^{\mu}=p^{\mu}_{3}-p^{\mu}_{1}=(0,\mathbf{Q}).

(2.35)

In the Breit frame, the momentum transfer has only spatial components,

Q^{\mu}=p^{\mu}_{3}-p^{\mu}_{1}=(0,\mathbf{Q}),\qquad\mathbf{p}_{3}=-\mathbf{p}_{1}=\frac{\mathbf{Q}}{2}.

(2.36)

We further denote

\mathbf{p}_{3}=-\mathbf{p}_{1}=\frac{\mathbf{Q}}{2},\qquad q=|\mathbf{Q}|,\qquad\hat{q}=\frac{\mathbf{Q}}{|\mathbf{Q}|}.

(2.37)

The matrix element evaluated in the Breit frame is denoted by

\mathcal{M}^{\mu\nu\cdots}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\mathcal{M}^{\mu\nu\cdots}(p_{1},p_{3};a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.38)

Once the Breit frame has been chosen, the angular dependence of the elastic matrix element is fully encoded in the direction
\hat{q}
. The Lorentz indices of the operator can then be separated into time and spatial components and classified according to
\mathrm{SO}(3)
. In the following discussion, we focus mainly on a spatial component with indices
i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n}
. The corresponding matrix element can be expanded in a finite set of independent
\mathrm{SO}(3)
tensor structures,

\mathcal{M}^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n}}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{r}F_{r}(q^{2})\,T^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n}}_{r}(\hat{q};a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.39)

The tensor structure
T^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n}}_{r}(\hat{q};a_{1},a_{3})
contains the dependence on the direction
\hat{q}
and on the external spin projections, while the scalar coefficient
F_{r}(q^{2})
is the corresponding form factor.
The scalar coefficient
F_{r}(q^{2})
depends on the chosen tensor basis rather than on the choice of reference frame. For an elastic form factor, the Breit frame is kinematically equivalent to the COM frame formed by the two external momenta. Therefore, when the same basis is used, the same coefficient
F_{r}(q^{2})
is obtained in both descriptions. The explicit form of
F_{r}(q^{2})
depends on the chosen tensor basis, but the physical information contained in the full matrix element is basis independent.

In the Condon-Shortley phase convention, crossing the initial spin state

|p_{3};ja_{3}\rangle
to the final side converts its spin ket into the corresponding bra state,

|j,a_{3}\rangle\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad(-1)^{j-a_{3}}\langle j,-a_{3}|.

(2.40)

To make the spin structure explicit, we rewrite the matrix element in a crossed-channel form by moving the initial particle to the final side. Keeping the crossing phase explicitly, the matrix element can be written as

\mathcal{M}^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n}}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=(-1)^{j-a_{3}}\,\langle p_{1},\bar{p}_{3};ja_{1},j(-a_{3})|\mathcal{O}^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n}}|0\rangle.

(2.41)

Next, the final state and the crossed initial state can be coupled through CGCs to obtain the total spin part
S
. In the
\mathrm{SO}(3)
representation, two particles with spin
j
are decomposed by the standard angular momentum decomposition into irreducible
\mathrm{SO}(3)
representations. The corresponding decomposition and CGC can be written as

j\otimes j=\bigoplus_{S=0}^{2j}S,\qquad(-1)^{j-a_{3}}C^{S,a_{S}}_{j,a_{1};j,-a_{3}},

(2.42)

where the phase from crossing the initial spin state to the final side is kept in front of the CGC.

Equivalently, this crossed-channel coupling can also be expressed in terms of the rotation generators associated with the spin of the external particles. The spin coupling part
\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}
is defined as

\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})\equiv\mathcal{N}_{jS}\,(-1)^{j-a_{3}}C^{S,a_{S}}_{j,a_{1};j,-a_{3}},\qquad S=0,1,\ldots,2j,

(2.43)

where the constants
\mathcal{N}_{jS}
are used for the normalization matched to the rotation generators associated with the spin of the external particles.

Next, the corresponding spin generators are given. For the external particles with spin-
\frac{1}{2}
,
the matrix elements of the generators are

(J^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.44)

where

\sigma^{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma^{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma^{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(2.45)

Their spherical components
5
5

5

The detailed relation between the spherical basis and the tensor basis is given in Appendix
A
.
are

\sigma_{0}=\sigma^{z},\qquad\sigma_{+1}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\sigma^{x}+i\sigma^{y}),\qquad\sigma_{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\sigma^{x}-i\sigma^{y}).

(2.46)

For the external particles with spin-
1
, the matrix elements of the generators are

S^{x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad S^{y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-i&0\\
i&0&-i\\
0&i&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad S^{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(2.47)

The corresponding spherical components are

S_{0}=S^{z},\qquad S_{+1}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(S^{x}+iS^{y}),\qquad S_{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(S^{x}-iS^{y}).

(2.48)

With these conventions, for the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
case, the corresponding coupling gives total spin
S=0
and
S=1
. The corresponding spin coupling structures can be written as

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=0:\qquad\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})=\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.49)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=1:\qquad\Sigma^{(1)}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})=(\sigma_{m})_{a_{1}a_{3}},\qquad m=0,\pm 1,

where
\Sigma^{(1)}_{m}
is the spherical spin component. The Cartesian spin vector is

\Sigma^{(1),i}_{\rm cart}(a_{1},a_{3})\equiv\sum_{m=-1}^{1}\bar{e}^{i}_{m}\Sigma^{(1)}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})=(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.50)

where
\bar{e}^{i}_{m}
denotes the dual spherical basis vector used to convert spherical components into tensor components, and it can be obtained from Eq. (
A.2
).

For spin-
1
, the corresponding coupling gives total spin
S=0
,
S=1
and
S=2
. The corresponding spin coupling structures can be written as

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=0:\qquad\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})=\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.51)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=1:\qquad\Sigma^{(1)}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})=(S_{m})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=2:\qquad\Sigma^{(2)}_{M}(a_{1},a_{3})=Q^{(2)}_{M,a_{1}a_{3}},

where
(S_{m})_{a_{1}a_{3}}
and
Q^{(2)}_{M}
is the spherical spin component. The Cartesian form are

\Sigma^{(1),i}_{\rm cart}(a_{1},a_{3})\equiv\sum_{m=-1}^{1}\bar{e}^{i}_{m}\Sigma^{(1)}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})=(S^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.52)

and

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(2),ij}_{\rm cart}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{M=-2}^{2}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}\Sigma^{(2)}_{M}(a_{1},a_{3})

(2.53)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{2}\{S^{i},S^{j}\}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\mathbf{S}^{2}\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle=Q^{ij}_{a_{1}a_{3}},

where
\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}
denotes the dual spherical basis tensor used to convert spherical components into tensor components.
Therefore for spin-
1
the three crossed-channel sectors
S=0,1,2
correspond to the monopole, dipole, and quadrupole spin structures.

The orbital part is considered next. In the Breit frame, the external kinematic dependence is encoded in the magnitude
q
and the direction
\hat{q}
. For a fixed
\mathrm{SO}(3)
component of the operator, the angular dependence can be expanded in spherical harmonics
Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})
, which carry orbital angular momentum
L
. In Cartesian form, a rank-
L
spherical harmonic is equivalent to the symmetric traceless part of
L
factors of
\hat{q}^{i}
projected onto the spherical-component tensor basis,

Y^{L}_{M}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{(2L+1)!!}{4\pi L!}}\,e^{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}_{LM}\,\hat{q}^{\langle i_{1}}\cdots\hat{q}^{i_{L}\rangle},

(2.54)

where angular brackets denote the symmetric traceless projection. Thus the spherical label
(L,M)
and the Cartesian symmetric traceless tensor carry the same irreducible
\mathrm{SO}(3)
representation.

For
L=0
,

Y^{0}_{0}(\hat{q})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}.

(2.55)

For
L=1
, the spherical harmonics are the spherical components of the vector

\hat{q}^{i}
, up to the standard normalization,

Y^{1}_{M}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,e^{i}_{M}\hat{q}^{i}.

(2.56)

For
L=2
, the relevant Cartesian object is the symmetric traceless quadrupole

Q^{ij}(\hat{q})\equiv\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}.

(2.57)

The five
L=2
spherical harmonics are the five spherical components of this
symmetric traceless tensor, equivalently the contractions of
Q^{ij}
with the
basis tensors in Eq. (
A.5
). Explicitly,

Y^{2}_{0}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{5}{16\pi}}\,(3(\hat{q}^{z})^{2}-1)=\sqrt{\frac{45}{16\pi}}\,Q^{zz},

(2.58)

Y^{2}_{+1}(\hat{q})=-\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{z}(\hat{q}^{x}+i\hat{q}^{y})=-\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,(Q^{zx}+iQ^{zy}),

(2.59)

Y^{2}_{-1}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{z}(\hat{q}^{x}-i\hat{q}^{y})=\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,(Q^{zx}-iQ^{zy}),

(2.60)

and

Y^{2}_{+2}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{15}{32\pi}}\,(\hat{q}^{x}+i\hat{q}^{y})^{2}=\sqrt{\frac{15}{32\pi}}\,\left(Q^{xx}-Q^{yy}+2iQ^{xy}\right),

(2.61)

Y^{2}_{-2}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{15}{32\pi}}\,(\hat{q}^{x}-i\hat{q}^{y})^{2}=\sqrt{\frac{15}{32\pi}}\,\left(Q^{xx}-Q^{yy}-2iQ^{xy}\right).

(2.62)

Having identified the spin and orbital parts, the multipole expansion is organized by coupling the crossed-channel spin
S
to the orbital angular momentum
L
according to
L\otimes S\to J
. For a given component with definite total angular momentum
J
, the corresponding crossed-channel three-point structure is introduced as

\mathcal{A}^{J,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{L,S}F^{(J)}_{LS}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{J,M}_{L,m;S,a_{S}}Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.63)

The tensor
\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}
is the irreducible spin tensor defined in Eq. (
2.43
). It is a spherical component in spin space. The coefficients
F^{(J)}_{LS}(q^{2})
are the form factors in the
LS
basis. They are scalar functions of
q^{2}
and contain the dynamical information associated with the operator insertion in a channel of definite total angular momentum
J
, orbital angular momentum
L
, and crossed-channel spin
S
. By construction, all nontrivial angular dependence is carried by the spherical harmonics.
The CGC
C^{J,M}_{L,m;S,a_{S}}
couples the orbital part and the spin part into total angular momentum
J
with projection
M
.

Once the spherical amplitude
\mathcal{A}^{J,M}
is obtained, the corresponding Cartesian tensor matrix element is reconstructed by attaching the appropriate dual Cartesian reconstruction tensor,

\mathcal{M}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{J,M}\mathbb{T}^{\,i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}_{J,M}\,\mathcal{A}^{J,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.64)

where the tensor
\mathbb{T}^{\,i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}_{J,M}
converts the spherical component
\mathcal{A}^{J,M}
into the Cartesian indices carried by the matrix element, which can be obtained from Eqs. (
A.4
) and (
A.6
).
For a scalar operator component, there is no Cartesian index and only
J=0
contributes, so that
\mathbb{T}_{0,0}=1
. For a vector operator component,
J=1
, and the reconstruction tensor is the dual spherical basis vector
\mathbb{T}^{i}_{1,M}=\bar{e}^{i}_{M}
. The Cartesian vector matrix element is therefore reconstructed as

\mathcal{M}^{i}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{M=-1}^{1}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}\,\mathcal{A}^{1,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.65)

Therefore, Eq. (
2.64
) is simply the conversion from irreducible spherical components to Cartesian tensor components.

2.3
Zemach tensor expansion

We now rewrite the multipole expansion in the Zemach tensor formulation
[
51
,
52
]
. In the Breit frame, the orbital dependence is described by symmetric traceless tensors built from the direction
\hat{q}
, while the spin dependence is described by tensors constructed from the rest frame wave functions of the external states. The corresponding
LS
decomposition is therefore realized entirely at the tensor level.

For the matrix element of an operator component with definite total angular momentum
J
under spatial rotations, the orbital dependence is carried by the direction
\hat{q}
, and the rank-
L
Zemach tensor is defined by

Y^{(L)}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}(\hat{q})\equiv\hat{q}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{q}_{i_{L}}-\text{trace part},

(2.66)

where
Y^{(L)}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}
denotes the Cartesian symmetric traceless tensor built from
\hat{q}
, in contrast to the spherical harmonic
Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})
in spherical basis.
For the first few values of
L
, one has

Y^{(0)}=1,\qquad Y^{(1)}_{i}=\hat{q}_{i},\qquad Y^{(2)}_{ij}=\hat{q}_{i}\hat{q}_{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}.

(2.67)

We now turn to the spin part. For a particle of spin-
j
, let
\chi^{(j)}_{A}(a)
denote the rest frame wave function with canonical spin projection
a=-j,-j+1,\dots,j
, where
A
collectively denotes the spin indices in the chosen tensor realization. For integer spin,
\chi^{(j)}_{A}
may be realized as a symmetric traceless Cartesian tensor of rank
j
. For half-integer spin, it is realized as the corresponding spinor or spinor-tensor wave function.

The Cartesian spin tensors are obtained by coupling the initial and final rest-frame wave functions into irreducible
\mathrm{SO}(3)
tensors. For a given total spin
S
, we define

\Sigma^{(S),k_{1}\cdots k_{S}}_{\rm cart}(a_{1},a_{3})=\big[\chi^{(j)\dagger}(a_{1})\otimes\chi^{(j)}(a_{3})\big]^{(S),k_{1}\cdots k_{S}},

(2.68)

namely the irreducible Cartesian rank-
S
tensor obtained by coupling the initial and final rest-frame wave functions. Since
j\otimes j=\bigoplus\limits_{S=0}^{2j}S
, the allowed spin tensors are labeled by
S=0,1,\dots,2j
.

Within the Zemach tensor method, the
LS
multipole expansion of the matrix element of an operator component with definite total angular momentum
J
can be written as

\mathcal{M}^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{J}}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{L,S}F^{(J)}_{LS}(q^{2})\big[Y^{(L)}(\hat{q})\otimes\Sigma^{(S)}_{\rm cart}(a_{1},a_{3})\big]^{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{J}}.

(2.69)

The functions
F^{(J)}_{LS}(q^{2})
are the form factors in the
LS
basis. All angular dependence is carried by the Zemach tensors, while the dynamical information is encoded in the scalar coefficients
F^{(J)}_{LS}(q^{2})
.

We now specialize to the spin-
\frac{1}{2}\to\frac{1}{2}
case.
For spin-
\frac{1}{2}
, the rest-frame wave functions reduce to the two-component Pauli spinors

\chi_{+\frac{1}{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\chi_{-\frac{1}{2}}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(2.70)

Since
\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}=0\oplus 1
, the corresponding spin tensors are

\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})=\chi^{\dagger}_{a_{1}}\chi_{a_{3}},\qquad\Sigma^{(1),i}_{\rm cart}(a_{1},a_{3})=\chi^{\dagger}_{a_{1}}\sigma^{i}\chi_{a_{3}},

(2.71)

where
\sigma^{i}
are the Pauli matrices.

2.4
Comparison among three methods

2.4.1
Relation between multipoles and
LS
expansion

We now keep the same conventional electromagnetic multipole expansion, but rewrite it as a matrix element between the initial and final one-particle states. Thus the current components
J^{0}(\mathbf{q})
and
J^{i}(\mathbf{q})
are replaced by
\mathcal{M}^{0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})
and
\mathcal{M}^{i}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})
, and the multipole coefficients acquire the little-group indices of the external states. No new multipole basis is being introduced here; this is only a change of notation that keeps track of the spin labels and makes the comparison with the crossed-channel
LS
method direct. In the Breit frame, the time component is an
\mathrm{SO}(3)
scalar and is therefore expanded in ordinary spherical harmonics,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2j}\sum_{m=-k}^{k}C^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{*k}_{m}(\hat{q})

(2.72)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2j}\sum_{m=-k}^{k}(-1)^{m}C^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{k}_{-m}(\hat{q}),

(2.73)

where
k
denotes the conventional multipole rank. The spatial component of the matrix element transforms as an
\mathrm{SO}(3)
vector and is expanded in the same vector spherical harmonics used for the spatial current,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{i}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2j}\sum_{m=-k}^{k}\Big[L^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{(\mathrm{L})*\,i}_{km}(\hat{q})+E^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{(\mathrm{E})*\,i}_{km}(\hat{q})+M^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{(\mathrm{M})*\,i}_{km}(\hat{q})\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2j}\sum_{m=-k}^{k}(-1)^{m}\Big[L^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{(\mathrm{L})\,i}_{k,-m}(\hat{q})+E^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{(\mathrm{E})\,i}_{k,-m}(\hat{q})+M^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})\,Y^{(\mathrm{M})\,i}_{k,-m}(\hat{q})\Big].

(2.74)

Here
C^{k}_{m}
,
L^{k}_{m}
,
E^{k}_{m}
, and
M^{k}_{m}
are the same conventional charge,
longitudinal, electric, and magnetic multipoles defined above. The vector
spherical harmonics are also the same ones introduced in
Eqs. (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
); in particular,
for
k=0
only the longitudinal branch survives.

For the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
example below, only
k=0
and
k=1
can appear. We
therefore first write the ordinary spherical harmonics in terms of

q^{i}/q
. For
k=0
,

Y^{0}_{0}(\hat{q})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}.

(2.75)

For
k=1
,

Y^{1}_{0}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\frac{q^{z}}{q}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\hat{q}^{z},

(2.76)

Y^{1}_{+1}(\hat{q})=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\frac{q^{x}+iq^{y}}{q}=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}(\hat{q}^{x}+i\hat{q}^{y}),

(2.77)

and

Y^{1}_{-1}(\hat{q})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\frac{q^{x}-iq^{y}}{q}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}(\hat{q}^{x}-i\hat{q}^{y}).

(2.78)

Thus
q\nabla^{i}
removes the radial factor and projects onto the tangent plane of the sphere.

Now insert these results into the three vector harmonics
Eqs. (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
). For
k=0
, the ordinary harmonic
is constant, so

q\nabla^{i}Y^{0}_{0}=0.

(2.79)

The three vector harmonics are therefore

Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{00}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{i},\qquad Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{00}=0,\qquad Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{00}=0.

(2.80)

This is why the
k=0
spatial-current sector contains only the longitudinal
monopole
L_{0}
.

For
k=1
, it is compact to write

Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})=a^{j}_{m}\hat{q}^{j},

(2.81)

where

a^{j}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\delta^{jz},\qquad a^{j}_{+1}=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}(\delta^{jx}+i\delta^{jy}),\qquad a^{j}_{-1}=+\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}(\delta^{jx}-i\delta^{jy}).

(2.82)

The three
k=1
vector harmonics are

Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{1m}=\hat{q}^{i}\,a^{j}_{m}\hat{q}^{j},

(2.83)

Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{1m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,a^{j}_{m}\left(\delta^{ij}-\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}\right),

(2.84)

and

Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{1m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\epsilon^{ilj}\hat{q}^{l}a^{j}_{m}.

(2.85)

In particular, for
m=0
,

Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{10}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{z},\qquad Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{10}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\left(\delta^{iz}-\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{z}\right),

(2.86)

Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{10}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\,\epsilon^{ilz}\hat{q}^{l}.

(2.87)

For
m=+1
,

\displaystyle Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{1,+1}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}^{x}+i\hat{q}^{y}),

\displaystyle Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{1,+1}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{16\pi}}\,\left[\delta^{ix}+i\delta^{iy}-\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}^{x}+i\hat{q}^{y})\right],

\displaystyle Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{1,+1}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{16\pi}}\,\epsilon^{ilj}\hat{q}^{l}(\delta^{jx}+i\delta^{jy}).

(2.88)

For
m=-1
,

\displaystyle Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=+\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}^{x}-i\hat{q}^{y}),

\displaystyle Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=+\sqrt{\frac{3}{16\pi}}\,\left[\delta^{ix}-i\delta^{iy}-\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}^{x}-i\hat{q}^{y})\right],

\displaystyle Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{1,-1}

\displaystyle=+\sqrt{\frac{3}{16\pi}}\,\epsilon^{ilj}\hat{q}^{l}(\delta^{jx}-i\delta^{jy}).

(2.89)

Next, we consider the spin part in front of the vector spherical harmonic functions obtained from the matrix element decomposition. This part contains the spin information of the initial and final states. Therefore, it can be written as

X^{k}_{m}(q^{2};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};ja_{1}|X^{k}_{m}|p_{3};ja_{3}\rangle,\qquad X=C,L,E,M,

(2.90)

where
X^{k}_{m}
is an operator with angular momentum
k
. By using the Wigner-Eckart theorem, the above expression can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\langle p_{1};ja_{1}|X^{k}_{m}|p_{3};ja_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2j+1}}X_{k}(q^{2})\mathcal{S}^{k}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})

(2.91)

\displaystyle=C^{j,a_{1}}_{j,a_{3};k,m}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2j+1}}X_{k}(q^{2})

(2.92)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j-a_{3}}\left[\frac{2j+1}{2k+1}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}C^{k,-m}_{j,a_{3};j,-a_{1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2j+1}}X_{k}(q^{2}).

(2.93)

where the standard symmetry relation of the CGCs has
been used. This formula is simply a CGC multiplied by a scalar coefficient.
Therefore, we only need to identify the corresponding spin tensor basis.

For example, for the particle spin-
\frac{1}{2}
and
k=0
, it can be written as

\mathcal{S}^{0}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})\propto\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.94)

For
k=1
, it can be written as

\mathcal{S}^{1}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})\propto(\sigma^{z})_{a_{1}a_{3}},\qquad\mathcal{S}^{1}_{+1}(a_{1},a_{3})\propto-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\sigma^{x}+i\sigma^{y})_{a_{1}a_{3}},\qquad\mathcal{S}^{1}_{-1}(a_{1},a_{3})\propto\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\sigma^{x}-i\sigma^{y})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.95)

Because the spin tensors in Eqs. (
2.94
) and (
2.95
) have only been fixed up to normalization, the following spin-dependent structures are written up to the corresponding absorbed scalar coefficients. Keeping only
k=0
and
k=1
, the charge component is therefore

\mathcal{M}^{0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})\propto C_{0}(q^{2})\,\mathcal{S}^{0}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})Y^{0}_{0}(\hat{q})+C_{1}(q^{2})\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})Y^{1}_{-m}(\hat{q}).

(2.96)

The
k=0
structure is

\mathcal{S}^{0}_{0}Y^{0}_{0}\propto\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.97)

whereas the
k=1
structure is

\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{m}Y^{1}_{-m}\ \propto\ (\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.98)

up to the normalization convention for the spherical components. These are the
km
-dependent parts multiplying the reduced form factors
C_{0}(q^{2})
and
C_{1}(q^{2})
.

For the spatial current, the same factorization gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{i}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\propto L_{0}(q^{2})\,\mathcal{S}^{0}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{00}(\hat{q})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{m}(a_{1},a_{3})\Big[L_{1}(q^{2})Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{1,-m}(\hat{q})+E_{1}(q^{2})Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{1,-m}(\hat{q})+M_{1}(q^{2})Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{1,-m}(\hat{q})\Big].

(2.99)

The
km
-dependent structures multiplying the reduced form factors are

L_{0}:\quad\mathcal{S}^{0}_{0}Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{00}\propto\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\hat{q}^{i}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.100)

L_{1}:\quad\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{m}Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{1,-m}\ \propto\ \hat{q}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.101)

E_{1}:\quad\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{m}Y^{(\mathrm{E})i}_{1,-m}\ \propto\ \left[\sigma^{i}-\hat{q}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.102)

and

M_{1}:\quad\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{m}\mathcal{S}^{1}_{m}Y^{(\mathrm{M})i}_{1,-m}\ \propto\ \bigl(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q}\bigr)^{i}_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.103)

The complete conventional spin-
\frac{1}{2}
multipole content before imposing parity or current conservation is

J^{0}:\quad C_{0},\ C_{1},\qquad J^{i}:\quad L_{0},\ L_{1},\ E_{1},\ M_{1},

(2.104)

where each symbol denotes a reduced form factor multiplying the corresponding
km
structure displayed above.

Spin-
\frac{1}{2}
example

For elastic spin-
\frac{1}{2}
matrix elements, the crossed-channel spin can take only
S=0,1
. Therefore the possible spin tensors, after undoing the crossing convention, are the scalar spin structure and the vector spin structure,

S=0:\quad\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},\qquad S=1:\quad(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.105)

In the
LS
basis, the orbital harmonic
Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})
is coupled directly to these spin tensors. In the conventional basis, one first builds
C/L/E/M
harmonics and only then restores the spin dependence.

First consider the charge density
J^{0}
. Since it is a scalar component, the
total angular momentum is
J=0
and the
LS
condition is
L=S
. The two
allowed channels are

(L,S)=(0,0),\qquad(1,1),

(2.106)

which give

(0,0):\quad\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},\qquad(1,1):\quad(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.107)

Thus the one-to-one identification in the charge sector is

C_{0}\ \longleftrightarrow\ (L,S)=(0,0)\ \longleftrightarrow\ \delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.108)

C_{1}\ \longleftrightarrow\ (L,S)=(1,1)\ \longleftrightarrow\ (\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.109)

Here
C_{0}
uses the constant harmonic
Y^{0}_{0}
and the spin scalar

\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}
;
C_{1}
uses the dipole harmonic
Y^{1}_{m}\sim\hat{q}^{m}
and
the spin vector
\sigma^{m}
, coupled to a scalar. This is exactly the

(1,1)
channel found in Eq. (
2.134
).

Now consider the spatial current
J^{i}
. In the
LS
basis its vector index is
restored only at the end by
\mathbb{T}^{i}_{1,M}=\bar{e}^{i}_{M}
, so the relevant
component has total angular momentum
J=1
. The allowed spin-
\frac{1}{2}

LS
channels are therefore

(L,S)=(1,0),\qquad(0,1),\qquad(1,1),\qquad(2,1).

(2.110)

After the Cartesian vector index is restored with

\mathbb{T}^{i}_{1,M}=\bar{e}^{i}_{M}
, these four channels become

(1,0):\quad\hat{q}^{i}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.111)

(0,1):\quad(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.112)

(1,1):\quad\bigl(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q}\bigr)^{i}_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.113)

(2,1):\quad\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.114)

The conventional multipoles give the same four-dimensional vector space, but
they reach it in a different order. Let us spell out each case.

The longitudinal monopole
L_{0}
comes from the
k=0
vector harmonic computed
in Eq. (
2.80
),

Y^{(\mathrm{L})i}_{00}\propto\hat{q}^{i}.

(2.115)

Since
k=0
carries no spin tensor beyond
S=0
, this is precisely the

LS
channel
(L,S)=(1,0)
:

L_{0}\ \longleftrightarrow\ (L,S)=(1,0)\ \longleftrightarrow\ \hat{q}^{i}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.116)

The magnetic dipole
M_{1}
comes from the
k=1
magnetic vector harmonic. From
Eq. (
2.11
), it is the cross product of
\hat{q}
with the angular
gradient of the dipole harmonic. When the spin-
1
tensor

\sigma^{j}
is restored, the only rank-one antisymmetric coupling is

i\epsilon^{ijk}\hat{q}_{j}\sigma_{k}
. This is the
LS
channel
(L,S)=(1,1)
:

M_{1}\ \longleftrightarrow\ (L,S)=(1,1)\ \longleftrightarrow\ \bigl(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q}\bigr)^{i}_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.117)

The remaining two conventional dipoles,
E_{1}
and
L_{1}
, are more subtle and
this is where the comparison with the
LS
basis is most useful. The
electric and longitudinal
k=1
vector harmonics are not single
LS
channels.
They are the two orthogonal combinations of the two symmetric spin-dependent
channels

(0,1):\quad\sigma^{i},\qquad(2,1):\quad 3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}.

(2.118)

Explicitly,

E_{1}\ \longleftrightarrow\ \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,(0,1)-\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\,(2,1)\ \longleftrightarrow\ \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,\sigma^{i}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\bigr),

(2.119)

L_{1}\ \longleftrightarrow\ \sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\,(0,1)+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,(2,1)\ \longleftrightarrow\ \sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\,\sigma^{i}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\bigr).

(2.120)

Therefore the precise statement is the following. The conventional

C/L/E/M
multipoles and the
LS
coefficients do not label the tensors in the
same order, but they span the same space.
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
,
L_{0}
, and
M_{1}

each select one
LS
channel, while
E_{1}
and
L_{1}
are the two fixed
orthogonal linear combinations of
(0,1)
and
(2,1)
.

Conventional multipole

Current component

LS
channel

Cartesian structure

C_{0}

J^{0}

(L,S)=(0,0)

\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}

C_{1}

J^{0}

(L,S)=(1,1)

(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}

L_{0}

J^{i}

(L,S)=(1,0)

\hat{q}^{i}\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}

M_{1}

J^{i}

(L,S)=(1,1)

\bigl(i\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q}\bigr)^{i}_{a_{1}a_{3}}

E_{1}

J^{i}

\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,(0,1)-\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\,(2,1)

fixed combination of
\sigma^{i}
and

3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}

L_{1}

J^{i}

\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\,(0,1)+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,(2,1)

orthogonal combination of the same two spin-dependent structures

Table 1:
Explicit relation between the conventional charge, longitudinal,
electric, and magnetic multipoles and the crossed-channel
LS
basis for
spin-
\frac{1}{2}
matrix elements. The entries are given up to normalization
and phase conventions.

Equivalently, the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
matrix elements may be written as

\mathcal{M}^{0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=f_{0}(q^{2})\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{0}(q^{2})\,(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.121)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{i}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=f_{1}(q^{2})\,\hat{q}^{i}\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{1}(q^{2})\,(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}+h_{1}(q^{2})\,\bigl(i(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+k_{1}(q^{2})\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.122)

The charge multipoles
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
span the two-dimensional scalar space in
Eq. (
2.121
). Likewise,
L_{0}
,
L_{1}
,
E_{1}
, and
M_{1}

span the four-dimensional vector space in
Eq. (
2.122
).

General result

The spin-
\frac{1}{2}
example shows the essential relation between the traditional electromagnetic multipoles and the crossed-channel
LS
basis. The two bases do not use the same coupling order, but they span the same irreducible
\mathrm{SO}(3)
tensor space. This statement extends directly to arbitrary external spin-
j
.

For the spin
j
, the crossed-channel total spin can take

S=0,1,\ldots,2j.

(2.123)

Thus the conventional multipole rank-
k
is identified with the crossed-channel spin rank,

S=k,\qquad 0\leq k\leq 2j.

(2.124)

The charge multipole is a scalar component of the current. Therefore the orbital part and the spin part must be coupled to total angular momentum zero. This fixes

C_{k}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad(L,S)=(k,k).

(2.125)

For the spatial current, the Cartesian vector index is treated differently in the two bases. In the conventional basis, the orbital harmonic is first combined with the vector index to form the longitudinal, electric, and magnetic vector harmonics. In the
LS
basis, the orbital harmonic is first coupled with the crossed-channel spin tensor, and the Cartesian vector index is restored only at the end. Therefore the difference between the two descriptions is only a recoupling of the same
\mathrm{SO}(3)
tensors.

The magnetic multipole selects the middle orbital branch and corresponds to one
LS
channel,

M_{k}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad(L,S)=(k,k).

(2.126)

By contrast, the longitudinal and electric multipoles do not correspond to single
LS
channels. For each
k\geq 1
, they span the two-dimensional space generated by

(L,S)=(k-1,k),\qquad(L,S)=(k+1,k).

(2.127)

More explicitly, up to the same phase and normalization conventions used above,

L_{k}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad\sqrt{\frac{k}{2k+1}}\,(k-1,k)+\sqrt{\frac{k+1}{2k+1}}\,(k+1,k),

(2.128)

and

E_{k}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad\sqrt{\frac{k+1}{2k+1}}\,(k-1,k)-\sqrt{\frac{k}{2k+1}}\,(k+1,k).

(2.129)

The relative signs depend on the phase convention for the vector spherical harmonics, but the invariant statement is that
L_{k}
and
E_{k}
are two orthogonal linear combinations of the same two
LS
channels.

For
k=0
, the branch
(k-1,k)
is absent. Therefore the spatial current has only the longitudinal monopole,

L_{0}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad(L,S)=(1,0),

(2.130)

while
E_{0}
and
M_{0}
are absent.

The general correspondence is summarized in Table
2
.

Conventional multipole

Current component

Conventional structure

Crossed-channel
LS
content

C_{k}

J^{0}

charge multipole of rank
k

single scalar channel
(L,S)=(k,k)

L_{0}

J^{i}

longitudinal monopole

single vector channel
(L,S)=(1,0)

M_{k}

J^{i}

magnetic multipole of rank
k

single vector channel
(L,S)=(k,k)

L_{k}

J^{i}

longitudinal multipole of rank
k

linear combination of
(k-1,k)
and
(k+1,k)

E_{k}

J^{i}

electric multipole of rank
k

orthogonal linear combination of
(k-1,k)
and
(k+1,k)

Table 2:
Relation between the conventional electromagnetic multipoles and the
crossed-channel
LS
basis for arbitrary external spin
j
. The allowed
multipole ranks satisfy
0\leq k\leq 2j
. For
k=0
, only
C_{0}
and
L_{0}
are
present.

Therefore, the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
result is not a special feature of Pauli
matrices. It is the lowest-spin realization of a general recoupling relation.
The conventional electromagnetic multipoles classify the current by charge,
longitudinal, electric, and magnetic components, while the
LS
basis
classifies the same structures by orbital angular momentum and
crossed-channel total spin.

2.4.2
Spin-
\frac{1}{2}
matrix elements

The general
LS
construction is now illustrated for spin
j=\frac{1}{2}
particles. In this case, the crossed-channel total spin can only take the values
S=0,1
. The corresponding spin tensors follow from Eq. (
2.43
). For spin
j=\frac{1}{2}
, the spherical spin components reduce to

\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}=\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},\qquad\Sigma^{(1)}_{m}=(\sigma_{m})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.131)

Scalar component

For a scalar operator component,
J=0
and

\mathcal{M}_{(0)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};\frac{1}{2}a_{1}|\mathcal{O}_{(0)}|p_{3};\frac{1}{2}a_{3}\rangle.

(2.132)

The condition
L\otimes S\to 0
requires
L=S
, so the allowed channels are

(L,S)=(0,0),\qquad(1,1).

(2.133)

Substituting these two channels into the general
LS
formula Eq. (
2.63
) gives

\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=F^{(0)}_{00}(q^{2})\,Y^{0}_{0}(\hat{q})\,\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})+F^{(0)}_{11}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\,\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.134)

For a scalar component, Eq. (
2.64
) has
\mathbb{T}_{0,0}=1
, so the matrix element is obtained directly from
\mathcal{A}^{0,0}
. The first channel is immediate:

Y^{0}_{0}\,\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.135)

Here the
S=0
spin tensor is the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
specialization of Eq. (
2.43
). For the second channel, the CGC coupling
1\otimes 1\to 0
is

C^{0,0}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}=\frac{(-1)^{1-m}}{\sqrt{3}}\,\delta_{m,-a_{S}}.

(2.136)

Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\,\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\sum_{m=-1}^{1}(-1)^{1-m}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\,\Sigma^{(1)}_{-m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\,(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.137)

where in the last step we used the rank-one case of Eq. (
2.54
) for the orbital part and
Eq. (
2.43
) for the rank-one spin part. Hence the scalar component has the form

\mathcal{M}_{(0)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=f_{(0)}(q^{2})\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(0)}(q^{2})\,(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.138)

The exact coefficient relation is therefore

f_{(0)}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}F^{(0)}_{00}(q^{2}),\qquad g_{(0)}(q^{2})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}F^{(0)}_{11}(q^{2}).

(2.139)

If parity is imposed, the first structure is parity even, while the second is parity odd.

Vector component

For a vector operator component,
J=1
and

\mathcal{M}^{i}_{(1)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};\frac{1}{2}a_{1}|\mathcal{O}^{i}_{(1)}|p_{3};\frac{1}{2}a_{3}\rangle.

(2.140)

The condition
L\otimes S\to 1
in Eq. (
2.63
) gives four allowed channels,

(L,S)=(1,0),\qquad(0,1),\qquad(1,1),\qquad(2,1).

(2.141)

Keeping only these channels, Eq. (
2.63
) becomes

\mathcal{A}^{1,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{(L,S)}F^{(1)}_{LS}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{L,m;S,a_{S}}Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})\,\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}),

(2.142)

where the sum runs over the four channels above. The Cartesian vector is obtained as the special case of Eq. (
2.64
),

\mathcal{M}^{i}_{(1)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{M=-1}^{1}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}\,\mathcal{A}^{1,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3}),

(2.143)

with
\bar{e}^{i}_{M}
defined in Eq. (
A.2
). The four channels are now reduced separately.

For
(L,S)=(1,0)
, the spin tensor is the scalar tensor in Eq. (
2.43
), and
C^{1,M}_{1,m;0,0}=\delta_{Mm}
. Using Eq. (
2.143
) to attach the vector basis
\bar{e}^{i}_{M}
gives

\sum_{M,m}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}C^{1,M}_{1,m;0,0}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}\ =\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{i}\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.144)

where the last equality uses Eq. (
2.54
) at
L=1
. For
(L,S)=(0,1)
, the orbital part is a scalar and
C^{1,M}_{0,0;1,a_{S}}=\delta_{Ma_{S}}
. Therefore,

\sum_{M,a_{S}}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}C^{1,M}_{0,0;1,a_{S}}Y^{0}_{0}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}\ =\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\,(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.145)

where the spherical components of
\Sigma^{(1)}
, defined in Eq. (
2.43
), reconstruct the Cartesian Pauli matrix after contraction with the dual basis vector in Eq. (
A.2
).

For
(L,S)=(1,1)
, the coupling of two vectors to total
J=1
gives the antisymmetric vector product. Applying the CGC coupling in Eq. (
2.63
) and then using Eq. (
2.143
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{M,m,a_{S}}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}C^{1,M}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}\

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\,i\epsilon^{ijk}\hat{q}_{j}(\sigma_{k})_{a_{1}a_{3}}

(2.146)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}i(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{i}.

(2.147)

For
(L,S)=(2,1)
, the
L=2
harmonic is represented by the symmetric traceless tensor
Q^{ij}=\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}
defined in Eq. (
2.57
), and its spherical components are obtained from the rank-two basis in Eq. (
A.5
). Coupling this rank-two tensor with the rank-one spin tensor to total
J=1
gives the vector obtained by one contraction,

\sum_{M,m,a_{S}}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}C^{1,M}_{2,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{2}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}\ =-\frac{1}{\sqrt{12\pi}}\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.148)

This is the same structure as the one-contraction form, since

\left(\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\sigma_{j}=\frac{1}{3}\left[3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\right].

(2.149)

Therefore, the vector component matrix element can be written as

\mathcal{M}^{i}_{(1)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=f_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\hat{q}^{i}\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(1)}(q^{2})\,(\sigma^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}+h_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\bigl(i(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}+k_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.150)

where

f_{(1)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}F^{(1)}_{10}(q^{2}),\qquad g_{(1)}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}F^{(1)}_{01}(q^{2}),

(2.151)

h_{(1)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}F^{(1)}_{11}(q^{2}),\qquad k_{(1)}(q^{2})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{12\pi}}F^{(1)}_{21}(q^{2}).

(2.152)

The four terms in Eq. (
2.150
) correspond respectively to the channels
(1,0)
,
(0,1)
,
(1,1)
, and
(2,1)
.

Tensor component

We finally consider a general rank-two Cartesian tensor component,

\mathcal{M}^{ij}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};\frac{1}{2}a_{1}|\mathcal{O}^{ij}|p_{3};\frac{1}{2}a_{3}\rangle.

(2.153)

A general rank-two spatial tensor is reducible under rotations and decomposes as

1\otimes 1=0\oplus 1\oplus 2.

(2.154)

In Cartesian form, this decomposition can be written as

\mathcal{M}^{ij}=\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\mathcal{M}_{(0)}+\epsilon^{ijk}\mathcal{M}^{k}_{(1)}+\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{(2)}.

(2.155)

The three irreducible components are

\mathcal{M}_{(0)}=\delta_{ij}\mathcal{M}^{ij},\qquad\mathcal{M}^{k}_{(1)}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{kij}\mathcal{M}^{ij},\qquad\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{M}^{ij}+\mathcal{M}^{ji})-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\mathcal{M}_{(0)}.

(2.156)

Thus, the trace gives the
J=0
part, the antisymmetric part gives the
J=1
part, and the symmetric traceless part gives the
J=2
part. These three components are evaluated below in the
LS
basis.

The
J=0
trace part is exactly the scalar component already computed in Eq. (
2.138
),

\mathcal{M}_{(0)}=f_{(0)}(q^{2})\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(0)}(q^{2})(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.157)

According to Eq. (
2.155
), its contribution to the original tensor is therefore

\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{J=0}=\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\left[f_{(0)}(q^{2})\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(0)}(q^{2})(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}\right].

(2.158)

The
J=1
part is the antisymmetric component. From Eq. (
2.156
), its Cartesian tensor contribution is
\epsilon^{ijk}\mathcal{M}^{k}_{(1)}
. Using the vector result in Eq. (
2.150
) gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{J=1}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{ijk}\Big[f_{(1)}(q^{2})\hat{q}^{k}\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(1)}(q^{2})(\sigma^{k})_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+h_{(1)}(q^{2})\bigl(i(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{k}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}+k_{(1)}(q^{2})\bigl(3\hat{q}^{k}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\sigma^{k}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}\Big].

(2.159)

This term is antisymmetric in
i,j
, as required.

It remains to compute the
J=2
symmetric traceless part. With
S=0,1
, the condition
L\otimes S\to 2
in Eq. (
2.63
) gives

(L,S)=(2,0),\qquad(1,1),\qquad(2,1),\qquad(3,1).

(2.160)

Therefore,

\mathcal{A}^{2,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{(L,S)}F^{(2)}_{LS}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{2,M}_{L,m;S,a_{S}}Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})\,\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}),

(2.161)

where the sum runs over these four channels. The Cartesian symmetric traceless tensor is obtained from Eq. (
2.64
) using the dual rank-two basis in Eq. (
A.6
),

\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{(2)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{M=-2}^{2}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}\,\mathcal{A}^{2,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.162)

For
(L,S)=(2,0)
, the spin tensor is the scalar tensor in Eq. (
2.43
), and the CGC reduces to

C^{2,M}_{2,m;0,0}=\delta_{Mm}.

(2.163)

Thus Eq. (
2.162
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{M,m}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}C^{2,M}_{2,m;0,0}Y^{2}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{M}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}Y^{2}_{M}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,Q^{ij}(\hat{q})\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.164)

where the last equality uses Eqs. (
A.5
) and (
2.57
). Hence

(2,0):\qquad\big[Y^{(2)}\otimes\Sigma^{(0)}\big]^{(2)ij}\ =\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,\left(\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.165)

For
(L,S)=(1,1)
, the required CGCs are those for
1\otimes 1\to 2
. They are

C^{2,M}_{1,m;1,a}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{(1+M)(2+M)}{12}},&a=1,\quad m=M-1,\\[6.0pt]
\sqrt{\frac{(2-M)(2+M)}{6}},&a=0,\quad m=M,\\[6.0pt]
\sqrt{\frac{(1-M)(2-M)}{12}},&a=-1,\quad m=M+1,\\[6.0pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.166)

Substitution of Eq. (
2.166
) into Eq. (
2.162
), together with the rank-one relations in Eqs. (
2.54
) and (
2.43
), gives the standard symmetric traceless product of two Cartesian vectors,

\displaystyle\sum_{M,m,a}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}C^{2,M}_{1,m;1,a}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{q}^{i}\sigma^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}\sigma^{i}\right)-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.167)

Therefore,

(1,1):\qquad\big[Y^{(1)}\otimes\Sigma^{(1)}\big]^{(2)ij}\ =\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{q}^{i}\sigma^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}\sigma^{i}\right)-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.168)

For
(L,S)=(2,1)
, the orbital tensor is the quadrupole
Q^{ij}
in Eq. (
2.57
). The CGCs for
2\otimes 1\to 2
can be written as

C^{2,M}_{2,m;1,a}=\begin{cases}-\sqrt{\frac{(3-M)(2+M)}{12}},&a=1,\quad m=M-1,\\[6.0pt]
\frac{M}{\sqrt{6}},&a=0,\quad m=M,\\[6.0pt]
\sqrt{\frac{(3+M)(2-M)}{12}},&a=-1,\quad m=M+1,\\[6.0pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.169)

These antisymmetric coefficients project the product of the rank-two orbital tensor and the spin vector onto

\displaystyle\sum_{M,m,a}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}C^{2,M}_{2,m;1,a}Y^{2}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{5}{16\pi}}\,i\left[\epsilon^{ikl}Q^{jk}(\hat{q})\sigma^{l}+\epsilon^{jkl}Q^{ik}(\hat{q})\sigma^{l}\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{5}{16\pi}}\,i\left[\hat{q}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{i}\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.170)

where the
\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}
part of
Q^{ij}
drops out after the contraction with
\epsilon^{ijk}
. Thus,

(2,1):\qquad\big[Y^{(2)}\otimes\Sigma^{(1)}\big]^{(2)ij}\ =-\sqrt{\frac{5}{16\pi}}\,i\left[\hat{q}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{i}\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.171)

This tensor is symmetric, and it is traceless because
\hat{q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})=0
.

For
(L,S)=(3,1)
, the orbital part is the rank-three symmetric traceless tensor
\hat{q}^{\langle i}\hat{q}^{j}\hat{q}^{k\rangle}
, obtained from the general formula in Eq. (
2.54
). The CGCs for
3\otimes 1\to 2
are

C^{2,M}_{3,m;1,a}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{(4-M)(3-M)}{42}},&a=1,\quad m=M-1,\\[6.0pt]
-\sqrt{\frac{(3-M)(3+M)}{21}},&a=0,\quad m=M,\\[6.0pt]
\sqrt{\frac{(4+M)(3+M)}{42}},&a=-1,\quad m=M+1,\\[6.0pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.172)

Substitution into Eq. (
2.162
) selects the unique contraction of the rank-three symmetric traceless orbital tensor with the spin vector:

\displaystyle\sum_{M,m,a}\bar{e}^{ij}_{2M}C^{2,M}_{3,m;1,a}Y^{3}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{\sqrt{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{\langle i}\hat{q}^{j}\hat{q}^{k\rangle}(\sigma_{k})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.173)

Therefore,

(3,1):\qquad\big[Y^{(3)}\otimes\Sigma^{(1)}\big]^{(2)ij}\ =-\frac{5}{\sqrt{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{\langle i}\hat{q}^{j}\hat{q}^{k\rangle}(\sigma_{k})_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.174)

Equivalently,

\hat{q}^{\langle i}\hat{q}^{j}\hat{q}^{k\rangle}\sigma_{k}=\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\frac{1}{5}\left[\hat{q}^{i}\sigma^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}\sigma^{i}+\delta^{ij}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right],

(2.175)

which is symmetric and traceless in
i,j
.

The
J=2
part is therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{(2)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=f_{(2)}(q^{2})\left(\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+g_{(2)}(q^{2})\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{q}^{i}\sigma^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}\sigma^{i}\right)-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+h_{(2)}(q^{2})\left[i\hat{q}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{j}+i\hat{q}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\times\hat{q})^{i}\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+k_{(2)}(q^{2})\left[\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})-\frac{1}{5}\left(\hat{q}^{i}\sigma^{j}+\hat{q}^{j}\sigma^{i}+\delta^{ij}(\hat{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right)\right]_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.176)

The four terms correspond respectively to the
LS
channels
(2,0)
,
(1,1)
,
(2,1)
, and
(3,1)
. The corresponding exact coefficient map is

f_{(2)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}F^{(2)}_{20}(q^{2}),\qquad g_{(2)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}F^{(2)}_{11}(q^{2}),

(2.177)

h_{(2)}(q^{2})=-\sqrt{\frac{5}{16\pi}}F^{(2)}_{21}(q^{2}),\qquad k_{(2)}(q^{2})=-\frac{5}{\sqrt{8\pi}}F^{(2)}_{31}(q^{2}).

(2.178)

Combining Eqs. (
2.158
),
(
2.159
), and (
2.176
), the most general rank-two spin-
\frac{1}{2}
tensor matrix element is

\mathcal{M}^{ij}=\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{J=0}+\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{J=1}+\mathcal{M}^{ij}_{(2)}.

(2.179)

2.4.3
Spin-
1
matrix elements

Matrix elements between spin-
1
states are considered next. The construction is parallel to the spin
j=\frac{1}{2}
case, while the crossed-channel spin space contains one additional irreducible sector. In the crossed channel,

1\otimes 1=0\oplus 1\oplus 2.

(2.180)

The label
S
denotes the total spin obtained by coupling the final spin-
1
state to the crossed initial spin-
1
state,

(-1)^{1-a_{3}}C^{S,a_{S}}_{1,a_{1};1,-a_{3}}.

(2.181)

The three possibilities have the direct interpretation

S=0:\ \text{Monopole},\qquad S=1:\ \text{Dipole},\qquad S=2:\ \text{Quadrupole}.

(2.182)

The the rotation generators in the spin-
1
representation are denoted by
\mathbf{S}
, with matrix elements
(S^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}
in the canonical spin basis
a_{1},a_{3}=-1,0,1
. The explicit matrices can be obtained from Eq. (
2.47
).

Scalar component

For the scalar component
\mathcal{O}_{(0)}
, the matrix element is

\mathcal{M}_{(0)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};1a_{1}|\mathcal{O}_{(0)}|p_{3};1a_{3}\rangle.

(2.183)

Since this operator component is a rotational scalar, the total angular momentum is
J=0
. The crossed-channel amplitude is

\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{L,S}F^{(0)}_{LS}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{L,m;S,a_{S}}Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.184)

For spin
j=1
, the crossed-channel spin can take the values

S=0,\ 1,\ 2.

(2.185)

The scalar condition
L\otimes S\to 0
again requires
L=S
, so that the allowed channels are

(L,S)=(0,0),\qquad(1,1),\qquad(2,2).

(2.186)

The amplitude therefore becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=F^{(0)}_{00}(q^{2})\,C^{0,0}_{0,0;0,0}Y^{0}_{0}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(0)}_{11}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(0)}_{22}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{2,m;2,a_{S}}Y^{2}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(2)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.187)

The three channels reduce to

(0,0):\qquad Y^{0}_{0}\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.188)

(1,1):\qquad\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}(\mathbf{S}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.189)

and

(2,2):\qquad\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{0,0}_{2,m;2,a_{S}}Y^{2}_{m}\Sigma^{(2)}_{a_{S}}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\,\hat{q}_{i}\hat{q}_{j}Q^{ij}_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.190)

After the spherical tensors are converted to Cartesian form, the scalar matrix element can be written as

\mathcal{M}_{(0)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=f_{(0)}(q^{2})\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(0)}(q^{2})\,(\mathbf{S}\cdot\hat{q})_{a_{1}a_{3}}+h_{(0)}(q^{2})\,\hat{q}_{i}\hat{q}_{j}Q^{ij}_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.191)

where

f_{(0)}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}F^{(0)}_{00}(q^{2}),\qquad g_{(0)}(q^{2})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}F^{(0)}_{11}(q^{2}),\qquad h_{(0)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}F^{(0)}_{22}(q^{2}).

(2.192)

Vector component

For a vector component
\mathcal{O}^{i}_{(1)}
, the matrix element is

\mathcal{M}^{i}_{(1)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\langle p_{1};1a_{1}|\mathcal{O}^{i}_{(1)}|p_{3};1a_{3}\rangle.

(2.193)

Since
\mathcal{O}^{i}_{(1)}
transforms as a vector under rotations, the total
angular momentum is
J=1
. The corresponding crossed-channel amplitude is

\mathcal{A}^{1,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{L,S}F^{(1)}_{LS}(q^{2})\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{L,m;S,a_{S}}Y^{L}_{m}(\hat{q})\Sigma^{(S)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.194)

For spin-
1
,
S=0,1,2
. The condition

L\otimes S\to 1

(2.195)

then gives the seven allowed channels

\begin{gathered}(L,S)=(1,0),\\
(L,S)=(0,1),\qquad(1,1),\qquad(2,1),\\
(L,S)=(1,2),\qquad(2,2),\qquad(3,2).\end{gathered}

(2.196)

Correspondingly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{1,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=F^{(1)}_{10}\sum_{m}C^{1,M}_{1,m;0,0}Y^{1}_{m}\Sigma^{(0)}_{0}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(1)}_{01}\sum_{a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{0,0;1,a_{S}}Y^{0}_{0}\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(1)}_{11}\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{1,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(1)}_{21}\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{2,m;1,a_{S}}Y^{2}_{m}\Sigma^{(1)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(1)}_{12}\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{1,m;2,a_{S}}Y^{1}_{m}\Sigma^{(2)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(1)}_{22}\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{2,m;2,a_{S}}Y^{2}_{m}\Sigma^{(2)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+F^{(1)}_{32}\sum_{m,a_{S}}C^{1,M}_{3,m;2,a_{S}}Y^{3}_{m}\Sigma^{(2)}_{a_{S}}(a_{1},a_{3}),

(2.197)

where the
q^{2}
of the form factors and the
\hat{q}
of the spherical harmonics have been suppressed on the right-hand
side. The Cartesian matrix element is again obtained from

\mathcal{M}^{i}_{(1)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})=\sum_{M=-1}^{1}\bar{e}^{i}_{M}\,\mathcal{A}^{1,M}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3}).

(2.198)

After this spherical-to-Cartesian conversion, using the Cartesian spin tensors
in Eq. (
2.47
), the
seven channels in Eq. (
2.196
) become

(1,0):\qquad\big[Y^{(1)}\otimes\Sigma^{(0)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,\hat{q}^{i}\,\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.199)

(0,1):\qquad\big[Y^{(0)}\otimes\Sigma^{(1)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\,(S^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.200)

(1,1):\qquad\big[Y^{(1)}\otimes\Sigma^{(1)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}\,i\epsilon^{ijk}\hat{q}_{j}(S_{k})_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.201)

(2,1):\qquad\big[Y^{(2)}\otimes\Sigma^{(1)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =-\frac{1}{\sqrt{12\pi}}\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\mathbf{S})-S^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.202)

(1,2):\qquad\big[Y^{(1)}\otimes\Sigma^{(2)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =-\frac{3}{\sqrt{20\pi}}\,Q^{ij}_{a_{1}a_{3}}\hat{q}_{j},

(2.203)

(2,2):\qquad\big[Y^{(2)}\otimes\Sigma^{(2)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =-\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,i\epsilon^{ijk}\hat{q}_{j}\hat{q}_{l}Q^{kl}_{a_{1}a_{3}},

(2.204)

and

(3,2):\qquad\big[Y^{(3)}\otimes\Sigma^{(2)}\big]^{(1)i}\ =\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}\,\left[\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}_{j}\hat{q}_{k}-\frac{1}{5}\left(\delta^{i}_{j}\hat{q}_{k}+\delta^{i}_{k}\hat{q}_{j}\right)\right]Q^{jk}_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.205)

In the last line the possible term proportional to

\hat{q}^{i}\delta_{jk}Q^{jk}
has vanished because
Q^{jk}
is traceless.
The spin-
1
vector matrix element is therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{i}_{(1)}(\mathbf{q};a_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=f_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\hat{q}^{i}\delta_{a_{1}a_{3}}+g_{(1)}(q^{2})\,(S^{i})_{a_{1}a_{3}}+h_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\bigl(i(\mathbf{S}\times\hat{q})^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad+k_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\bigl(3\hat{q}^{i}(\hat{q}\cdot\mathbf{S})-S^{i}\bigr)_{a_{1}a_{3}}+u_{(1)}(q^{2})\,Q^{ij}_{a_{1}a_{3}}\hat{q}_{j}

\displaystyle\quad+v_{(1)}(q^{2})\,i\epsilon^{ijk}\hat{q}_{j}\hat{q}_{l}Q^{kl}_{a_{1}a_{3}}+w_{(1)}(q^{2})\,\left[\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}_{j}\hat{q}_{k}-\frac{1}{5}\left(\delta^{i}_{j}\hat{q}_{k}+\delta^{i}_{k}\hat{q}_{j}\right)\right]Q^{jk}_{a_{1}a_{3}}.

(2.206)

The exact coefficient map is

f_{(1)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}F^{(1)}_{10}(q^{2}),\qquad g_{(1)}(q^{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}F^{(1)}_{01}(q^{2}),\qquad h_{(1)}(q^{2})=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}F^{(1)}_{11}(q^{2}),

(2.207)

k_{(1)}(q^{2})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{12\pi}}F^{(1)}_{21}(q^{2}),\qquad u_{(1)}(q^{2})=-\frac{3}{\sqrt{20\pi}}F^{(1)}_{12}(q^{2}),\qquad v_{(1)}(q^{2})=-\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}F^{(1)}_{22}(q^{2}),

(2.208)

w_{(1)}(q^{2})=\sqrt{\frac{15}{8\pi}}F^{(1)}_{32}(q^{2}).

(2.209)

The first four structures are the direct spin-
1
analogues of the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
vector structures, with
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
replaced by the spin-
1
generators
\mathbf{S}
. The last three are new quadrupole structures, which arise because the spin-
1
crossed channel contains the additional
S=2
sector.

3
Canonical spinor formalism

In the previous section, the multipole expansion and the
LS
coupling were formulated in the Breit frame. Only in this special frame does the momentum transfer become purely spatial, so that the matrix element can be organized directly under the three dimensional rotation group and the orbital and spin degrees of freedom admit the usual nonrelativistic
LS
interpretation. In a general reference frame, Lorentz boosts mix the time and spatial components of tensor operators and also rotate the spin degrees of freedom through Wigner rotations. As a result, different multipole structures are generally mixed, and the simple nonrelativistic
LS
decomposition is no longer manifest. Helicity form factors provide another useful parametrization of matrix elements, but the helicity basis ties the spin projection to the momentum direction and therefore does not display an explicit separation between orbital angular momentum and spin. A covariant tensor construction can restore Lorentz covariance, but the
LS
interpretation is not always transparent in that form.

The purpose of this section is to introduce a canonical spinor formulation of the covariant
LS
construction. In this formulation, Lorentz covariance is carried by the spinor indices, while the spin coupling is performed in the little group space. This separation allows one to keep the
LS
organization explicit while constructing Lorentz covariant matrix element bases. More concretely, a matrix element of a local operator in a Lorentz representation
(j_{L},j_{R})
can be converted into an auxiliary massive three point amplitude, and the complete canonical spinor
LS
basis of that amplitude can then be translated back into a complete basis of matrix elements. We first introduce the spinor variables and wave function conventions, then describe the direct
LS
coupling form and illustrate it with low spin examples. Finally, we compare the resulting structures with the nonrelativistic
LS
and multipole bases discussed in section
2
.

3.1
Spinor helicity formalism and relativistic wave functions

Before discussing the direct
LS
coupling form and the low spin examples, let us briefly summarize the spinor formalism with canonical particle states
[
1
,
24
]
. In this construction, the massive spinor helicity variables are taken as the basic building blocks, and the amplitude is decomposed into orbital and spin parts in the little group space.

For a massive particle, the four-momentum can be written in spinorial form as

p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=|p^{Q}_{\alpha}\rangle[p_{Q\dot{\alpha}}|.

(3.1)

The two-component canonical Wyel spinor variables are defined as

|p^{Q}\rangle_{\alpha}\equiv\lambda^{Q}_{\alpha},\qquad|p_{Q}]_{\dot{\alpha}}\equiv\tilde{\lambda}_{Q\dot{\alpha}},\qquad\langle p^{Q}|^{\alpha}\equiv\lambda^{Q\alpha},\qquad[p_{Q}|^{\dot{\alpha}}\equiv\tilde{\lambda}_{Q}^{\dot{\alpha}},

(3.2)

where
\alpha
and
\dot{\alpha}
are the undotted and dotted
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
Lorentz spinor indices, while
Q
is the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
little group index. The angle spinor
|p^{Q}\rangle_{\alpha}
is left handed, and the square spinor
|p_{Q}]_{\dot{\alpha}}
is right handed.

The Lorentz spinor indices are raised and lowered by the antisymmetric tensors
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}
,
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}
and
\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}
,
\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}
, while the little group indices are raised and lowered by
\epsilon^{QK}
and
\epsilon_{QK}
. Explicitly,

\lambda^{Q\alpha}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\lambda^{Q}_{\beta},\qquad\tilde{\lambda}_{Q}^{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\tilde{\lambda}_{Q\dot{\beta}},\qquad\lambda_{Q\alpha}=\epsilon_{KQ}\lambda^{K}_{\alpha},\qquad\tilde{\lambda}^{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}=\epsilon^{QK}\tilde{\lambda}_{K\dot{\alpha}}.

(3.3)

Accordingly, the basic spinor contractions are written as

\langle p^{Q}q^{K}\rangle,\qquad[p_{Q}q_{K}],\qquad\langle q^{Q}|p|k_{K}].

(3.4)

Using the canonical spinor variables introduced above, the wave function of a massive particle in the Lorentz representation
(s_{L},s_{R})
with spin-
s
can be written in a compact form. Before giving the general expression, it is helpful to begin with a few simple low spin examples:

\displaystyle u^{I}_{\alpha}(p)

\displaystyle\sim|p^{I}\rangle_{\alpha},

\displaystyle s=\tfrac{1}{2},\quad(s_{L},s_{R})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2},0\right),

(3.5)

\displaystyle u^{I}_{\dot{\beta}}(p)

\displaystyle\sim|p^{I}]_{\dot{\beta}},

\displaystyle s=\tfrac{1}{2},\quad(s_{L},s_{R})=\left(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\right),

\displaystyle u_{\alpha,\dot{\beta}}(p)

\displaystyle\sim p_{\alpha\dot{\beta}},

\displaystyle s=0,\quad(s_{L},s_{R})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\right),

\displaystyle u^{(I_{1}I_{2})}_{\alpha,\dot{\beta}}(p)

\displaystyle\sim|p^{(I_{1}}\rangle_{\alpha}\,[p^{I_{2})}|_{\dot{\beta}},

\displaystyle s=1,\quad(s_{L},s_{R})=\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\right),

\displaystyle u^{(I_{1}I_{2})}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(p)

\displaystyle\sim|p^{(I_{1}}\rangle_{(\alpha_{1}}\,|p^{I_{2})}\rangle_{\alpha_{2})},

\displaystyle s=1,\quad(s_{L},s_{R})=(1,0),

\displaystyle u^{(I_{1}I_{2})}_{\dot{\beta}_{1}\dot{\beta}_{2}}(p)

\displaystyle\sim|p^{(I_{1}}]_{(\dot{\beta}_{1}}\,|p^{I_{2})}]_{\dot{\beta}_{2})},

\displaystyle s=1,\quad(s_{L},s_{R})=(0,1),
